\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
such that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}:=\{(\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i}),y_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}\in{\bm{D}}_{N,N,C}
has
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}=\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}}
.
Note that we can apply the proposition since
d\geq N
.

Next, we apply
Proposition

B.19
to
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\in{\bm{D}}_{N,N,C}
with
m=\max\{\lceil 9N\rho^{2}\rceil,\lceil 600\log N\rceil\}
and
\alpha=1/5
.
Let us first check that the specified
m
satisfies the condition
24\alpha^{-2}\log N\leq m\leq N
for the proposition to be applied.

\alpha=1/5
and
m\geq 600\log N
ensure the first inequality
24\alpha^{-2}\log N\leq m
.
The second inequality
m\leq N
is decomposed into two parts.
Since
\rho\leq\frac{1}{3}
, we have
9N\rho^{2}\leq N
so that

\displaystyle\lceil 9N\rho^{2}\rceil\leq N.

(39)

Moreover,
600\log N\leq N
implies

\displaystyle\lceil 600\log N\rceil\leq N.

(40)

Gathering
Equations

30
and
31
proves
m\leq N
.
Additionally, as
N\geq 2
, we have
N\geq 600\log N\geq 600\log 2\geq 400
.
By
Lemma

B.22
, this implies
10\log N\leq N
.

By the
Proposition

B.19
, there exists 1-Lipschitz linear mapping
\phi:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
and
\beta>0
such that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}:=\{(\phi(\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i})),y_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}\in{\bm{D}}_{m,N,C}
satisfies

\displaystyle\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}}\geq\frac{4}{5}\beta\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}.

(41)

As
m\geq 600\log N\geq 10\log N
, the inequality
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{m}{N}}
is also satisfied by
Proposition

B.19
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{m}{N}}\overset{(a)}{\geq}\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\lceil 9N\rho^{2}\rceil}{N}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{9N\rho^{2}}{N}}=\frac{3}{2}\rho,

(42)

where (a) is by the definition of
m
.
Moreover, since
\varphi
and
\phi
are both 1-Lipschitz linear,
\phi\circ\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
is also 1-Lipschitz linear.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\phi(\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i}))\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert\phi(\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{0}}))\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert\phi(\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i}))-\phi(\varphi({\bm{0}}))\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{0}}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rVert_{2},

(43)

for all
i\in[N]
.
Hence, by letting
R:=\max_{i\in[N]}\{\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\}
, we have
\left\lVert\phi(\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i}))\right\rVert_{2}\leq R
for all
i\in[N]
.

Now, we set the first layer hidden matrix as the matrix
{\bm{W}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times d}
corresponding to
\phi\circ\varphi
under the standard basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
.
Moreover, set the first hidden layer bias as
{\bm{b}}:=2R{\bm{1}}=2R(1,1,\cdots,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle{\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}+{\bm{b}}\geq{\bm{0}},

(44)

for all
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}})
for all
i\in[N]
, where the comparison between two vectors are element-wise.
This is because for all
i\in[N],j\in[m]
and
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}},\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}})
, we have

\displaystyle({\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}+{\bm{b}})_{j}=({\bm{W}}{\bm{x}})_{j}+2R\geq 2R-\left\lVert{\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{2}\overset{(a)}{\geq}2R-\left\lVert{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{2}\overset{(b)}{\geq}2R-(R+\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}})\overset{(c)}{\geq}0,

where (a) is by
Equation

43
, (b) is by the triangle inequality, and (c) is due to
R\geq\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}}
.

We construct the first layer of the neural network as
f_{1}({\bm{x}}):=\sigma({\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}+{\bm{b}})
which includes the activation
\sigma
.
Next, we show that,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}:=\{(f_{1}({\bm{x}}_{i}),y_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}
satisfies

\displaystyle\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}}\geq\frac{6}{5}\rho\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}},

(45)

by the above properties.
This is because for
i\neq j
with
y_{i}\neq y_{j}
we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert f_{1}({\bm{x}}_{i})-f_{1}({\bm{x}}_{j})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sigma({\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}_{i}+{\bm{b}})-\sigma({\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}_{j}+{\bm{b}})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\left\lVert({\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}_{i}+{\bm{b}})-({\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}_{j}+{\bm{b}})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\phi(\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i}))-\phi(\varphi({\bm{x}}_{k}))\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}2\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\geq}2\times\frac{4}{5}\beta\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\geq}2\times\frac{4}{5}\times\frac{3}{2}\rho\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{12}{5}\rho\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\overset{(e)}{=}\frac{12}{5}\rho\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}},

where (a) is by
Equation

44
, (b) is by the definition of the
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}}
, (c) is by
Equation

41
, (d) is by
Equation

42
, and (e) is because
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}=\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}
.

By
Lemma

D.2
applied to
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}\in{\bm{D}}_{m,N,C}
, there exists
f_{2}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{m,P}
with
P=O(Nm^{2}(\log(\frac{m}{(\gamma^{\prime\prime})^{2}})+\log R^{\prime\prime}))
number of parameters that
\frac{5}{6}
-robustly memorize
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}
, where

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle:=(1-\frac{5}{6})\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}}\overset{(a)}{\geq}\frac{1}{6}\times\frac{12}{5}\rho\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}=\frac{2}{5}\rho\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}},

\displaystyle R^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle:=\max_{i\in[N]}\left\lVert f_{1}({\bm{x}}_{i})\right\rVert_{2}=\max_{i\in[N]}\left\lVert\sigma({\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}_{i}+{\bm{b}})\right\rVert_{2}=\max_{i\in[N]}\left\lVert{\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}_{i}+{\bm{b}}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i\in[N]}\left\lVert{\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert{\bm{b}}\right\rVert_{2}\leq 3R.

Here, (a) is by
Equation

45
.
Moreover,
f_{2}
has width
O(m)
and depth
\tilde{O}(N)
by
Lemma

D.2
.

Now, we claim that
f:=f_{2}\circ f_{1}

\rho
-robustly memorize
{\mathcal{D}}
.
For any
i\in[N]
, take
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}})
.
Then, by
Equation

44
, we have
f_{1}({\bm{x}})={\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}+{\bm{b}}
and
f_{1}({\bm{x}}_{i})={\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}_{i}+{\bm{b}}
so that

\displaystyle\left\lVert f_{1}({\bm{x}})-f_{1}({\bm{x}}_{i})\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert{\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}-{\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\rho\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}.

(46)

Moreover, putting
Equation

45
to
Equation

46
results
\left\lVert f_{1}({\bm{x}})-f_{1}({\bm{x}}_{i})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{5}{6}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}}
.
Since
f_{2}

\frac{5}{6}
-robustly memorize
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle f({\bm{x}})=f_{2}(f_{1}({\bm{x}}))=f_{2}(f_{1}({\bm{x}}_{i}))=y_{i}.

In particular,
f({\bm{x}})=y_{i}
for any
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}})
, concluding that
f
is a
\rho
-robust memorizer
{\mathcal{D}}
.

Regarding the number of parameters to construct
f
, notice that
f_{1}
consists of
(d+1)m=\tilde{O}(Nd\rho^{2})
parameters as
m=\tilde{O}(N\rho^{2})
.
f_{2}
consists of
\tilde{O}(Nm^{2})=\tilde{O}(N^{3}\rho^{4})
parameters.
Since the case V assumes
N<d
and large
\rho
regime deals with
\rho\geq\frac{1}{5\sqrt{d}}
, we have

\displaystyle Nd\rho^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 25Nd^{2}\rho^{4},

\displaystyle N^{3}\rho^{4}

\displaystyle\leq Nd^{2}\rho^{4}.

Therefore,
f
in total consists of
\tilde{O}(N^{3}\rho^{4}+Nd\rho^{2})=\tilde{O}(Nd^{2}\rho^{4})
number of parameters.
Moreover, since
f
has the same width as
f_{2}
and depth one larger than the depth of
f_{2}
, it follows that width of
f
is
O(m)=\tilde{O}(\rho^{2}N)=\tilde{O}(\rho^{2}d)
and the depth of
f
is
\tilde{O}(N)
.
This proves the theorem for the last case.

Bounding the Bit Complexity.

Now, let us analyze how we can implement the above network under a finite precision.
We have demonstrated that for every five cases, the depth
\tilde{O}(N)
and width
\rho^{2}d
suffice for constructing
f
that robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
.

Let
R:=\max_{i\in[N]}\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}+\mu
. Let
D=\tilde{O}(\rho^{2}d)
and
L=\tilde{O}(N)
denote the width and the depth of the constructed network.
Let
M
be the maximum absolute value of the parameter used for constructing
f
.
Finally let
\nu=0.1
.
By
Lemma

B.23
, there exists
\bar{f}
with
\tilde{O}(N)
bit complexity, that approximates
f
uniformly over
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{0}},R)
with error at most
\nu
, where
\tilde{O}(\cdot)
here hides polylogarithmic terms in
D,M,L
and
R
.
i.e.

\displaystyle\max_{\left\lVert{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{2}\leq R}\left|\bar{f}-f\right|\leq\nu.

Finally, to handle the error
\nu
, we use the floor function approximation from
Lemma

B.16
.
By
Lemma

B.16
with
\gamma=1/10
, there exists
\overline{\mathrm{Floor}}:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
with depth
n:=\lceil\log_{2}(C+1)\rceil
and width
5
such that
\overline{\mathrm{Floor}}(x)=\lfloor x\rfloor
for all
x\in[0,C+1)
with
x-\lfloor x\rfloor>\gamma=0.1
.
Moreover, the lemma guarantees that
\overline{\mathrm{Floor}}
can be exactly implemented with
O(n+\log 10)=O(\log C)=\tilde{O}(1)
bit complexity.

Thus, if
y^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}
satisfies
|y^{\prime}-y|\leq\nu=0.1
for some
y\in[C]
, then

\displaystyle y^{\prime}+0.5\in[y-0.1+0.5,y+0.1+0.5]\subseteq(y+0.1,y+1).

In particular,
\lfloor y^{\prime}+0.5\rfloor=y
and
\lfloor y^{\prime}+0.5\rfloor-(y^{\prime}+0.5)\in(0.1,1)
so that
\mathrm{Floor}(y^{\prime})=\lfloor y^{\prime}\rfloor=y
.
For
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu)
, we have
f({\bm{x}})=y_{i}
so that the approximation
\bar{f}
outputs
y^{\prime}=\bar{f}({\bm{x}})
such that

\displaystyle\left|\bar{f}({\bm{x}})-f({\bm{x}})\right|=\left|y^{\prime}-y_{i}\right|\leq\nu.

This shows
\overline{\mathrm{Floor}}(\bar{f}({\bm{x}}))=\overline{\mathrm{Floor}}(y^{\prime})=y_{i}
.
Moreover,
\overline{\mathrm{Floor}}\circ\bar{f}
can be implemented with parameters, width, and depth of the same scale as
\bar{f}
, and bit complexity
\tilde{O}(N)
.
This finishes the proof for the bit complexity.
∎

B.4
Lemmas for Lattice Mapping

Lemma B.15

(Avoiding Being Near Grid)
.

Let
N,d\in\mathbb{N}
and
{\bm{x}}_{1},\cdots,{\bm{x}}_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, there exists a translation vector
{\bm{b}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that:

\mathrm{dist}(x_{i,j}-b_{j},\mathbb{Z})\geq\frac{1}{2N},\quad\forall i\in[N],j\in[d],

i.e., the translated points
\{{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{b}}\}_{i\in[N]}
are coordinate-wise
\frac{1}{2N}
-far from the integer lattice.

Moreover, there exists
\bar{\bm{b}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
which has bit complexity
\lceil\log(6N)\rceil
and satisfies

\mathrm{dist}(x_{i,j}-b_{j},\mathbb{Z})\geq\frac{1}{3N},\quad\forall i\in[N],j\in[d].

Proof.

For each coordinate
j\in[d]
, consider the set
\{x_{i,j}\}_{i\in[N]}
of all
j
-th coordinate values.
For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let
\{x\}:=x-\lfloor x\rfloor
denote the fractional part of
x
. We consider the collection of fractional parts
\left\{\{x_{i,j}\}\right\}_{i\in[N]}
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume
0\leq\{x_{1,j}\}<\{x_{2,j}\}<\cdots<\{x_{N,j}\}<1
.

For each
j\in[d]
, define the maximum fractional gap
g_{j}\in[0,1)
as

g_{j}:=\max\left(\max_{i\in[N-1]}\left(\{x_{i+1,j}\}-\{x_{i,j}\}\right),\;1-\{x_{N,j}\}+\{x_{1,j}\}\right).

We claim:

g_{j}\geq\frac{1}{N},\textrm{ for all }j\in[d].

Suppose for a contradiction that
g_{j}<\frac{1}{N}
for some
j\in[d]
. Then, we have by the definition of
g_{j}
,

\displaystyle\{x_{N,j}\}-\{x_{1,j}\}=\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}(\{x_{i+1,j}\}-\{x_{i,j}\})\leq(N-1)g_{j}<\frac{N-1}{N},

(47)

\displaystyle 1-\{x_{N,j}\}+\{x_{1,j}\}\leq g_{j}<\frac{1}{N}\iff\{x_{N,j}\}-\{x_{1,j}\}>\frac{N-1}{N}.

(48)

Equations

47
and
48
lead to a contradiction, proving the claim.

Now, we define the translation of the
j
-th coordinate,
b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
, based on the location where the maximum
g_{j}
is attained. If the maximum in the definition of
g_{j}
occurs by the difference of some consecutive pair
(\{x_{i^{\prime},j}\},\{x_{i^{\prime}+1,j}\})
satisfying
\{x_{i^{\prime}+1,j}\}-\{x_{i^{\prime},j}\}=g_{j}
, we set

b_{j}=\frac{\{x_{i^{\prime},j}\}+\{x_{i^{\prime}+1,j}\}}{2}.

In this case,
\mathrm{dist}(x_{i^{\prime},j}-b_{j},{\mathbb{Z}})=\mathrm{dist}(x_{i^{\prime}+1,j}-b_{j},{\mathbb{Z}})=\frac{1}{2}g_{j}\geq\frac{1}{2N}
. For other
i
,
\mathrm{dist}(x_{i,j}-b_{j},{\mathbb{Z}})
is even larger by the order relation within
\{\{x_{i,j}\}\}_{i\in[N]}
.

Otherwise, if the maximum in the definition of
g_{j}
is attained as
1-\{x_{N,j}\}+\{x_{1,j}\}=g_{j}
, we define

b_{j}=\frac{(\{x_{N,j}\}-1)+\{x_{1,j}\}}{2}.

In this case,
\mathrm{dist}(x_{1,j}-b_{j},{\mathbb{Z}})=\mathrm{dist}(x_{N,j}-b_{j},{\mathbb{Z}})=\frac{1}{2}g_{j}\geq\frac{1}{2N}
. For other
i
,
\mathrm{dist}(x_{i,j}-b_{j},{\mathbb{Z}})
is even larger by the order relation within
\{\{x_{i,j}\}\}_{i\in[N]}
.

We define the full translation vector
{\bm{b}}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then the translated points
\{{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{b}}\}_{i\in[N]}
satisfy:

\mathrm{dist}(x_{i,j}-b_{j},\mathbb{Z})\geq\frac{1}{2N},\quad\text{for all }i\in[N],j\in[d].

Intuitively,
b_{j}
is chosen as the midpoint of the widest gap between fractional values, ensuring that all fractional parts after the translation are at least
\frac{g_{j}}{2}
away from the nearest integer. Therefore, the translated points are coordinate-wise
\frac{1}{2N}
-far from lattice points.

We define
\bar{\bm{b}}
such that each of its coordinates is equal to the first
\lceil\log(6N)\rceil
bits of the corresponding coordinate of
{\bm{b}}
. Then, for all
j\in[d]
, we have
|b_{j}-\bar{b}_{j}|\leq\frac{1}{2^{\log(6N)}}\leq\frac{1}{6N}
.
Using
\bar{\bm{b}}
with bit complexity
\lceil\log(6N)\rceil
, we can still ensure the distance
\frac{1}{3N}
from the lattice points.

\mathrm{dist}(x_{i,j}-\bar{b}_{j},\mathbb{Z})\geq\mathrm{dist}(x_{i,j}-b_{j},\mathbb{Z})-|b_{j}-\bar{b}_{j}|\geq\frac{1}{2N}-\frac{1}{6N}=\frac{1}{3N},\quad\text{for all }i\in[N],j\in[d].

∎

The following lemma shows that we can approximate the floor function using a logarithmic number of ReLU units with respect to the length of the interval of interest.

Lemma B.16

(Floor Function Approximation)
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\gamma\in(0,1)
, there exists an
n
-layer network
\overline{{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}}
with width 5 and
5n
ReLU units such that

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}}(x)=\lfloor x\rfloor\textrm{ for all }x\in[0,2^{n})\textrm{ such that }x-\lfloor x\rfloor>\gamma.

Moreover, if
\gamma=\frac{1}{t}
for some
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then
\overline{{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}}
can be exactly implemented with
2n+\log_{2}t
bit complexity under a fixed point precision.

Proof.

To reconcile the discontinuity of the floor function with the continuity of ReLU networks, we first define a discontinuous ideal building block that exactly replicates the floor function on the target interval
[0,2^{n})
.
We then approximate this building block using a continuous neural network with ReLU activations.

The ideal building block
\Delta
is defined as:

\displaystyle\Delta(x):=\begin{cases}2x&\textrm{ if }x\in(0,\frac{1}{2}],\\
2x-1&\textrm{ if }x\in(\frac{1}{2},1],\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define the function
\mathrm{Floor}_{n}
by:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}(x)=\Delta^{n}(-\frac{x}{2^{n}}+1)+x-1.

We will show by induction that
{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}=\lfloor x\rfloor
for all
x\in[0,2^{n})
.

For the base case
n=1
,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Floor}}_{1}(x)=\Delta(-\frac{x}{2}+1)+x-1=\begin{cases}2(-\frac{x}{2}+1)-1+x-1=0&\textrm{ if }x\in[0,1),\\
2(-\frac{x}{2}+1)+x-1=1&\textrm{ if }x\in[1,2),\\
0+x-1=x-1&\textrm{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

This proves the base case: for all
x\in[0,2)
, we have
\mathrm{Floor}_{1}(x)=\lfloor x\rfloor
.

For the inductive step, assume that
\mathrm{Floor}_{n}(x)=\lfloor x\rfloor
holds for all
x\in[0,\;2^{n})
.
We aim to prove that
\mathrm{Floor}_{n+1}(x)=\lfloor x\rfloor
for all
x\in[0,\;2^{n+1})
.

Recall that:

\displaystyle\Delta(-\frac{x}{2^{n+1}}+1)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}-\dfrac{x}{2^{n}}+1&\text{if }x\in[0,2^{n})&(\Leftrightarrow-\dfrac{x}{2^{n+1}}+1\in(\tfrac{1}{2},1]),\\
-\dfrac{x}{2^{n}}+2=-\dfrac{x-2^{n}}{2^{n}}+1&\text{if }x\in[2^{n},2^{n+1})&(\Leftrightarrow-\dfrac{x}{2^{n+1}}+1\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2}]),\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n+1}(x)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{n+1}(-\frac{x}{2^{n+1}}+1)+x-1

\displaystyle=\Delta^{n}(\Delta(-\frac{x}{2^{n+1}}+1))+x-1

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\Delta^{n}(-\frac{x}{2^{n}}+1)+x-1={\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}(x)=\lfloor x\rfloor&\textrm{ if }x\in[0,2^{n}),\\
\Delta^{n}(-\frac{x-2^{n}}{2^{n}}+1)+x-1={\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}(x-2^{n})+2^{n}=\lfloor x\rfloor&\textrm{ if }x\in[2^{n},2^{n+1}),\\
\Delta^{n}(0)+x-1=x-1&\textrm{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Therefore, by induction,

{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}=\lfloor x\rfloor\text{ for all }x\in[0,2^{n}).

Next, we define the
{\sigma}
approximation
\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}
of the discontinuous block
\Delta
as:

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}(x):=

\displaystyle 2\sigma(x)-\frac{1}{\gamma_{n}}\sigma\left(x-\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{n}\right)+\frac{1}{\gamma_{n}}\sigma\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\gamma_{n}}\sigma\left(x-1+\gamma_{n}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{n}}-2\right)\sigma\left(x-1\right),

(49)

where
\gamma_{n}=\frac{\gamma}{2^{n}}
.
Check
Figure

7
for an illustration of how
\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}
looks like on
[0,1]
.
It is straightforward to check that

\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}(x)=\Delta(x)\text{ for all }x\in[0,\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{n}]\cup[\frac{1}{2},1-\gamma_{n}].

x
y
\tfrac{1}{2}-\gamma_{n}
\tfrac{1}{2}
1-\gamma_{n}
1
1
y=\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}(x)

Figure 7
:
Plot of the ReLU-based approximation
\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}(x)
of the ideal discontinuous building block
\Delta(x)
on
[0,1]
.

We now explain why this approximation remains valid under recursive composition up to depth
n
.

Let us define the variable
x^{\prime}:=-\frac{x}{2^{n}}+1
, so that
x=2^{n}(1-x^{\prime})
and
x^{\prime}\in(0,1]
. Our target function is:

{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}(x)=\Delta^{n}(-\frac{x}{2^{n}}+1)+x-1=\Delta^{n}(x^{\prime})+x-1.

We are given the assumption
x-\lfloor x\rfloor>\gamma
, and we aim to express this in terms of
x^{\prime}
to ensure
\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}^{n}(x^{\prime})=\Delta^{n}(x^{\prime})
. We proceed step-by-step:

\displaystyle x-\lfloor x\rfloor>\gamma

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle 2^{n}(1-x^{\prime})-\lfloor 2^{n}(1-x^{\prime})\rfloor>\gamma

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle-2^{n}x^{\prime}-\lfloor-2^{n}x^{\prime}\rfloor>\gamma

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle-2^{n}x^{\prime}+\lceil 2^{n}x^{\prime}\rceil>\gamma

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle 2^{n}x^{\prime}<\lceil 2^{n}x^{\prime}\rceil-\gamma

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle 2^{n}x^{\prime}\in(\lceil 2^{n}x^{\prime}\rceil-1,\lceil 2^{n}x^{\prime}\rceil-\gamma)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle 2^{n}x^{\prime}\in\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(k-1,k-\gamma\right)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle x^{\prime}\in\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n}},\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n}}\right).

Since
x^{\prime}\in(0,1]
, we only need to consider
k\in[2^{n}]
, i.e.,

x^{\prime}\in\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n}},\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n}}\right).

We will now prove by induction on
n
the following statement:

\textrm{For any }\gamma\in(0,1),\,\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}^{n}(x)=\Delta^{n}(x)\text{ for }x\in\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n}},\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n}}\right).

For the base case
n=1
, by construction of
\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,1}}(x)
, we know
\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,1}}(x)=\Delta(x)
for all
x\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{\gamma}{2}]\cup[\tfrac{1}{2},1-\tfrac{\gamma}{2}]
, which contains the union
\bigcup_{k\in[2]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2},\frac{k-\gamma}{2}\right).
Hence the base case holds.

For the inductive step, assume the claim holds for
n
. We show it holds for
n+1
.
By using
\gamma/2
in place of
\gamma
for the inductive hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta_{\gamma/2,n}}^{n}(x)=\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}^{n}(x)=\Delta^{n}(x)\textrm{ for all }x\in\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n}},\frac{k-\gamma/2}{2^{n}}\right).

(50)

Let
x\in\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n+1}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n+1}},\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n+1}}\right)
. We analyze two cases based on
x\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
or
x\in[\frac{1}{2},1)
.

First, consider the case
x\in\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n+1}},\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n+1}}\right)\subset[0,\frac{1}{2})
. Then
x<\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n+1}}\leq\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{n+1}
, so
\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}(x)=2x
.
Let
y:=2x
. Then:

y\in\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n}},\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n}}\right)\subseteq\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n}},\frac{k-\gamma/2}{2^{n}}\right).

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}^{n+1}(x)=

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}^{n}(\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}(x))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}^{n}(2x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}^{n}(y)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}

\displaystyle\Delta^{n}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{n}(2x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{n+1}(x),

where the equation (a) follows by
Equation

50
.

Second, consider the case
x\in\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n+1}]\backslash[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n+1}},\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n+1}}\right)\subset[\frac{1}{2},1)
. Then
\frac{1}{2}\leq x<\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n+1}}\leq 1-\gamma_{n+1}
, so
\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}(x)=2x-1
.
Let
y:=2x-1
. Then:

y\in\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n+1}]\backslash[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-2^{n}-1}{2^{n}},\frac{k-2^{n}-\gamma}{2^{n}}\right)=\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n}},\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n}}\right)\subseteq\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n}},\frac{k-\gamma/2}{2^{n}}\right).

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}^{n+1}(x)=

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}^{n}(\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}(x))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}^{n}(2x-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n+1}}^{n}(y)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}

\displaystyle\Delta^{n}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{n}(2x-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{n+1}(x),

where the equation (a) follows by
Equation

50
.

Therefore, by induction, we have shown that for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
and any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}^{\,n}(x^{\prime})=\Delta^{\,n}(x^{\prime})\quad\text{for all }x^{\prime}\in\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n}},\;\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n}}\right).

We now define the ReLU-based floor approximation by

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}}(x):=\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}^{\,n}\left(-\frac{x}{2^{n}}+1\right)+x-1.

(51)

Recall that the ideal target function is given by

{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}(x)=\Delta^{\,n}\left(-\frac{x}{2^{n}}+1\right)+x-1.

Let us denote
x^{\prime}:=-\frac{x}{2^{n}}+1
.
When
x-\lfloor x\rfloor>\gamma
, the value
x^{\prime}
satisfies

x^{\prime}\in\bigcup_{k\in[2^{n}]}\left(\frac{k-1}{2^{n}},\;\frac{k-\gamma}{2^{n}}\right),

so that
\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}^{\,n}(x^{\prime})=\Delta^{\,n}(x^{\prime})
by the result above.
Therefore, we conclude:

\overline{{\mathrm{Floor}}}_{n}(x)={\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}(x)=\lfloor x\rfloor\quad\text{for all }x\in[0,2^{n})\text{ such that }x-\lfloor x\rfloor>\gamma.

Finally to prove the additional statement regarding the bit complexity, consider the case
\gamma=\frac{1}{t}
for some
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
. By
Equation

51
, the bit complexity to implement
\overline{{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}}
is upper bounded by
n
plus the bit complexity to implement
\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}
.
Now, it suffices to consider the bit complexity required to implement
\overline{\Delta_{\gamma,n}}
.
From
Section

B.4
, observe that
1/\gamma_{n}=2^{n}/\gamma=2^{n}\times t
for
\gamma=1/t
. Since
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
, this can be exactly implemented with
\log(2^{n}\times t)=n+\log_{2}t
bit complexity.
Thus,
\overline{{\mathrm{Floor}}_{n}}
can be implemented exactly with
2n+\log_{2}t
bit complexity.
∎

B.5
Dimension Reduction via Careful Analysis of the Johnson-Lindenstrauss Lemma

We begin with a lemma that states a concentration of the length of the projection.

Lemma B.17

(Lemma 15.2.2,
Matousek 2013
)
.

For a unit vector
{\bm{x}}\in S^{d-1}
, let

\displaystyle\phi({\bm{x}})=(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{m})

be the mapping of
{\bm{x}}
onto the subspace spanned by the first
m
coordinates. Consider
{\bm{x}}\in S^{d-1}
chosen uniformly at random. Then, there exists
\beta
such that
\left\lVert\phi({\bm{x}})\right\rVert_{2}
is sharply concentrated around
\beta
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}[\left\lVert\phi({\bm{x}})\right\rVert_{2}\geq\beta+t]\leq 2e^{-t^{2}d/2}\textrm{ and }{\mathbb{P}}[\left\lVert\phi({\bm{x}})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\beta-t]\leq 2e^{-t^{2}d/2},

where for
m\geq 10\log d
, we have
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{m}{d}}
.

Based on the above concentration inequality, we state the Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma, in a version which reflects the benefit on the ratio of the norm preserved when the projecting dimension increases.
The proof follows that of Theorem 15.2.1 in
Matousek 2013
with a slight modification.

Lemma B.18

(Strengthened Version of the Johnson-Lindenstrauss Lemma)
.

For
N\geq 2
, let
X\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be an
N
point set. Then, for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
24\alpha^{-2}\log N\leq m\leq d
, there exists a 1-Lipschitz linear mapping
\phi:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
and
\beta>0
such that

\displaystyle(1-\alpha)\beta\left\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\phi({\bm{x}})-\phi({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{2}\leq(1+\alpha)\beta\left\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2},

(52)

for all
{\bm{x}},{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in X
. Moreover,
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{m}{d}}
whenever
m\geq 10\log d
.

Proof.

If
{\bm{x}}={\bm{x}}^{\prime}
, the inequality trivially holds for any
\phi
.
Hence, it suffices to find
\phi
that satisfies
Equation

52
for all
{\bm{x}},{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in X
with
{\bm{x}}\neq{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
.
Consider a random
m
-dimensional subspace
L
, and
\phi
be a projection onto
L
.
For any fixed
{\bm{x}}\neq{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in X
,
Lemma

B.17
implies that
\left\lVert\phi(\frac{{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}}{\left\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}})\right\rVert_{2}
is concentrated around some constant
\beta
. i.e.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[\left\lVert\phi\left(\frac{{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}}{\left\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}}\right)\right\rVert_{2}\geq(1+\alpha)\beta\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}d/2}\overset{(a)}{\leq}2e^{-\alpha^{2}m/8}\overset{(b)}{\leq}2e^{-3\log N}=\frac{2}{N^{3}}\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{1}{N^{2}},

where we use
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{m}{d}}
at (a),
m\geq 24\alpha^{-2}\log N
at (b), and
N\geq 2
at (c). Similarly,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[\left\lVert\phi\left(\frac{{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}}{\left\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}}\right)\right\rVert_{2}\leq(1-\alpha)\beta\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N^{2}}.

By linearity of
\phi
, we have
\phi({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime})=\phi({\bm{x}})-\phi({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
. Taking the union bound over the two probability bounds above, the following event happens with probability at most
2/N^{2}
:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\phi({\bm{x}})-\phi({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{2}\geq(1+\alpha)\beta\left\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}\textrm{ or }\left\lVert\phi({\bm{x}})-\phi({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{2}\leq(1-\alpha)\beta\left\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}.

(53)

Next, we take a union bound over all
\frac{N(N-1)}{2}
pairs
{\bm{x}},{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in X
with
{\bm{x}}\neq{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
.
Then, the probability that
Equation

53
happens for any
{\bm{x}},{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in X
with
{\bm{x}}\neq{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
is at most
\frac{2}{N^{2}}\times\frac{N(N-1)}{2}=1-\frac{1}{N}<1
.
Hence, there exists a
m
-dimensional subspace
L
such that
Equation

53
does not hold for any pair of
{\bm{x}},{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in X
.
In other words, there exists a
m
-dimensional subspace
L
such that

\displaystyle(1-\alpha)\beta\left\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\phi({\bm{x}})-\phi({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{2}\leq(1+\alpha)\beta\left\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2},

for all
{\bm{x}}\neq{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
.
By
Lemma

B.17
,
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{m}{d}}
whenever
m\geq 10\log d
.
This concludes the lemma.
∎

Proposition B.19

(Lipschitz Projection with Separation)
.

For
N\geq 2
, let
{\mathcal{D}}=\{({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
.
For any
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
24\alpha^{-2}\log N\leq m\leq d
, there exists 1-Lipschitz linear mapping
\phi:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
and
\beta>0
such that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}:=\{(\phi({\bm{x}}_{i}),y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}\in{\bm{D}}_{m,N,C}
satisfies

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\geq(1-\alpha)\beta\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}.

In particular,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\in{\bm{D}}_{m,N,C}
whenever
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
.
Moreover,
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{m}{d}}
whenever
m\geq 10\log d
.

Proof.

Let
X=\{{\bm{x}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
.
By
Lemma

B.18
, there exists 1-Lipschitz linear mapping
\phi:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
and
\beta>0
such that

\displaystyle(1-\alpha)\beta\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\phi({\bm{x}}_{i})-\phi({\bm{x}}_{j})\right\rVert_{2}\leq(1+\alpha)\beta\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}

(54)

for all
i,j\in[N]
.

The inequality
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}\geq(1-\alpha)\beta\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}}
follows from the inequality from
Lemma

B.18
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\min\{\left\lVert\phi({\bm{x}}_{i})-\phi({\bm{x}}_{j})\right\rVert_{2}\,\mid\,i,j\in[N]\textrm{ and }y_{i}\neq y_{j}\}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}\frac{1}{2}\min\{(1-\alpha)\beta\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mid\,i,j\in[N]\textrm{ and }y_{i}\neq y_{j}\}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)\beta\times\frac{1}{2}\min\{\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mid\,i,j\in[N]\textrm{ and }y_{i}\neq y_{j}\}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)\beta\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}},

where we use
Equation

54
at (a).

We next show
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\in{\bm{D}}_{m,N,C}
whenever
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
.
To show this, we need to prove
\phi({\bm{x}}_{i})\neq\phi({\bm{x}}_{j})
for all
i\neq j
.
Since
1-\alpha>0
and
\beta>0
, we have
\left\lVert\phi({\bm{x}}_{i})-\phi({\bm{x}}_{j})\right\rVert_{2}\geq(1-\alpha)\beta\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}>0
whenever
{\bm{x}}_{i}\neq{\bm{x}}_{j}
.
Moreover,
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
indicates that
{\bm{x}}_{i}\neq{\bm{x}}_{j}
whenever
i\neq j
.
All together, we have
\phi({\bm{x}}_{i})\neq\phi({\bm{x}}_{j})
for all
i\neq j
so that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\in{\bm{D}}_{m,N,C}
.
∎

Lemma B.20

(Projection onto
\log
-scale Dimension)
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
.
Then, there exist an integer
m=\tilde{O}(\log N)
and a 1-Lipschitz linear map
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that the projected dataset
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}=\{(\phi({\bm{x}}_{i}),y_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}\in{\bm{D}}_{m,N,C}
satisfies the separation bound

\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\geq\frac{5}{12}\sqrt{\frac{m}{d}}\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}.

Proof.

Let
\alpha=1/6
and
m:=\min\{d,\max\{\lceil 24\alpha^{-2}\log N\rceil,\lceil 10\log d\rceil\}\}
, then
m=\tilde{O}(\log N)
.
We construct the linear mapping into
m
dimension by dividing the cases into
d<24\alpha^{-2}\log N
or
d\geq 24\alpha^{-2}\log N
.

For the case
d<24\alpha^{-2}\log N
, we have
d<\max\{\lceil 24\alpha^{-2}\log N\rceil,\lceil 10\log d\rceil\}
, and therefore
m=d
.
We consider the identity map
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}(=\mathbb{R}^{m})
, which is 1-Lipschitz.
We have
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}:=\{(\phi({\bm{x}}_{i}),y_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}=\{({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}={\mathcal{D}}
, so that
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}=\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}>\frac{5}{12}\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}=\frac{5}{12}\sqrt{\frac{m}{d}}\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}
.

Otherwise, for the case
d\geq 24\alpha^{-2}\log N
, we first observe that
m\leq d
.
Since
24\alpha^{-2}\log N\leq d
, we have

\displaystyle\lceil 24\alpha^{-2}\log N\rceil\leq d.

(55)

Additionally, as
N\geq 2
, we have
d\geq 24\alpha^{-2}\log N\geq 864\log 2\geq e^{4}
.
By
Lemma

B.22
, this implies
10\log d\leq d
and therefore

\displaystyle\lceil 10\log d\rceil\leq d.

(56)

By
Equations

55
and
56
, we have
\max\{\lceil 24\alpha^{-2}\log N\rceil,\lceil 10\log d\rceil\}\leq d
. Thus, it follows
m=\max\{\lceil 24\alpha^{-2}\log N\rceil,\lceil 10\log d\rceil\}\leq d
.
By
Proposition

B.19
with
\alpha=\frac{1}{6}
, there exists 1-Lipschitz linear mapping
\phi:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
and
\beta>0
such that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}=\{(\phi({\bm{x}}_{i}),y_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}
satisfies
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}\geq\frac{5}{6}\beta\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}
.
Since
m=\max\{\lceil 24\alpha^{-2}\log N\rceil,\lceil 10\log d\rceil\}\geq 10\log d
, the inequality
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{m}{d}}
is also satisfied by
Proposition

B.19
.
Therefore,
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}\geq\frac{5}{6}\beta\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}\geq\frac{5}{12}\sqrt{\frac{m}{d}}\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}
.

In both cases, we have 1-Lipschitz linear map
\phi
such that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}=\{(\phi({\bm{x}}_{i}),y_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}
has separation

\displaystyle\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}\geq\frac{5}{12}\sqrt{\frac{m}{d}}\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}.

∎

Proposition B.21

(Natural Projection of High Dimensional Data)
.

For
d\geq N
, let
{\mathcal{D}}=\{({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
.
Then, there exists 1-Lipschitz linear mapping
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
such that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}=\{(\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i}),y_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}\in{\bm{D}}_{N,N,C}
satisfies

\displaystyle\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}=\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}

Proof.

Consider the tall matrix
{\bm{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times N}
defined as

\displaystyle{\bm{X}}=\begin{bmatrix}|&|&\cdots&|\\
{\bm{x}}_{1}&{\bm{x}}_{2}&\cdots&{\bm{x}}_{N}\\
|&|&\cdots&|\end{bmatrix}.

(57)

Then
\dim{\mathrm{Col}}({\bm{X}})\leq N\leq d
.
Take any subspace
V
such that
{\mathrm{Col}}({\bm{X}})\subseteq V\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\dim V=N
, and let
{\mathcal{B}}=\{{\bm{v}}_{1},\cdots,{\bm{v}}_{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
V
.
Let
{\bm{V}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times N}
be the matrix whose columns consist of vectors in
{\mathcal{B}}
:

\displaystyle{\bm{V}}=\begin{bmatrix}|&|&\cdots&|\\
{\bm{v}}_{1}&{\bm{v}}_{2}&\cdots&{\bm{v}}_{N}\\
|&|&\cdots&|\end{bmatrix}.

Define
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
as
\varphi({\bm{x}})={\bm{V}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}
.
We first verify that
\varphi
is 1-Lipschitz.
For any
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, let
{\bm{x}}={\bm{x}}_{V}+{\bm{x}}_{V^{\perp}}
where
{\bm{x}}_{V}\in V
and
{\bm{x}}_{V^{\perp}}\in V^{\perp}
.
Then,
{\bm{x}}_{V}={\bm{V}}{\bm{z}}
for some
{\bm{z}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, as
{\bm{x}}_{V}\in{\mathrm{Col}}(V)
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle{\bm{V}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle={\bm{V}}^{\top}({\bm{x}}_{V}+{\bm{x}}_{V^{\perp}})

\displaystyle={\bm{V}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}_{V}+{\bm{0}}

\displaystyle={\bm{V}}^{\top}{\bm{V}}{\bm{z}}

\displaystyle={\bm{I}}_{N}{\bm{z}}

\displaystyle={\bm{z}}.

(58)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\bm{V}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{2}\overset{(a)}{=}\left\lVert{\bm{z}}\right\rVert_{2}\overset{(b)}{=}\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{V}\right\rVert_{2}\overset{(c)}{\leq}\left\lVert{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{2},

(59)

where (a) is by
Equation

58
, (b) is because
\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{V}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left\lVert\sum_{i\in[N]}z_{i}{\bm{v}}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i\in[N]}z_{i}^{2}=\left\lVert{\bm{z}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, and (c) is because
\left\lVert{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{V}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{V^{\perp}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
.
Moreover, whenever
{\bm{x}}\in V
, then the equality holds for (c) of
Equation

59
.
Therefore,
\left\lVert{\bm{V}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{2}
for all
{\bm{x}}\in V
.

Since
\varphi
is linear

\displaystyle\left\lVert\varphi({\bm{x}})-\varphi({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\varphi({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert{\bm{V}}^{\top}({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\left\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2},

where (a) is by
Equation

59
.
This shows that
\varphi
is 1-Lipschitz.

Next, for
i,j\in[N]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i})-\varphi({\bm{x}}_{j})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert{\bm{V}}^{\top}({\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j}\right\rVert_{2},

where the last equality holds because
{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j}\in{\mathrm{Col}}({\bm{X}})\subseteq V
.

This shows that

\displaystyle\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\min\{\left\lVert\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i})-\varphi({\bm{x}}_{j})\right\rVert_{2}\,\mid\,y_{i}\neq y_{j}\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\min\{\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mid\,y_{i}\neq y_{j}\}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\mathcal{D}}.

This shows that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
also has the desired property.
∎

Lemma B.22
.

For
t\geq e^{4}
, we have
t\geq 10\log t
.

Proof.

Define
u(t):=t-10\log t
on the domain
(0,\infty)
.
Then, for all
t>10
,

\displaystyle\frac{du}{dt}=1-\frac{10}{t}>0,

so that
u
is an increasing function on
(10,\infty)
.
In particular,

\displaystyle u(e^{4})=e^{4}-10\log(e^{4})=e^{4}-40\geq 0

This concludes that
u(t)\geq 0
for all
t\geq e^{4}
, or equivalently,
t\geq 10\log t
for all
t\geq e^{4}
.
∎

B.6
Lemmas for Bit Complexity

The following lemma bounds how much bit complexity is sufficient for implementing the parameters of the neural network in order to obtain the required precision of the output.
Note that we do not require the network to output scala values. i.e. the following lemma also applies to neural networks that output vectors.

Lemma B.23
.

Let
f
be a neural network of
P
parameters, depth
L
and width
D
in which the parameters have infinite precision.
Let
R\geq 1
be the radius of the domain in which we want to approximate
f
.
If all the parameters of
f
are bounded by some
M\geq 1
, then for any
0<\nu<1
, there exists
\bar{f}
, which is implemented with
P
parameters, depth
L
, width
D
, and
\tilde{O}(L)
bit complexity such that

\displaystyle\max_{\left\lVert{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{2}\leq R}\left\lVert\bar{f}-f\right\rVert_{2}\leq\nu,

where
\tilde{O}(\cdot)
hides a polylogarithmic dependency on
D,M,L,R
and
1/\nu
.

Proof.

Let
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be the neural network defined as

\displaystyle{\bm{a}}_{0}

\displaystyle={\bm{x}}

\displaystyle{\bm{a}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sigma({\bm{W}}_{\ell}{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}({\bm{x}})+{\bm{b}}_{\ell})\textrm{ for }\ell=1,2,\cdots,L-1

\displaystyle f({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle={\bm{W}}_{L}({\bm{a}}_{L-1})+{\bm{b}}_{L},

where
{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{\ell}\times d_{\ell-1}},{\bm{b}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{\ell}}
with
d_{\ell}\leq D
for all
\ell\in[L]
.
Although
{\bm{a}}_{\ell}
depends on
{\bm{x}}
for all
\ell=0,\cdots,L-1
, we omit
{\bm{x}}
in the notation.
Note that
d_{0}=d
.
Given that every elements of
{\bm{W}}_{\ell}
and
{\bm{b}}_{\ell}
are bounded by
M
, for any
0<\zeta\leq M
, there exists
\bar{{\bm{W}}}_{\ell}
and
\bar{{\bm{b}}}_{\ell}
that can be implemented with
\lceil\log_{2}(M/\zeta)\rceil
bit complexity in which

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bar{{\bm{W}}}_{\ell}-{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\zeta

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bar{{\bm{b}}}_{\ell}-{\bm{b}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\zeta.

Using the approximated parameters
\bar{{\bm{W}}}_{\ell}
and
\bar{{\bm{b}}}_{\ell}
, we recursively define
\bar{f}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, the finite-precision approximation of
f
.

\displaystyle\bar{{\bm{a}}}_{0}

\displaystyle={\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\bar{{\bm{a}}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sigma(\bar{{\bm{W}}}_{\ell}\bar{{\bm{a}}}_{\ell-1}({\bm{x}})+\bar{{\bm{b}}}_{\ell})\textrm{ for }\ell=1,2,\cdots,L-1

\displaystyle\bar{f}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\bar{{\bm{W}}}_{L}(\bar{{\bm{a}}}_{L-1})+\bar{{\bm{b}}}_{L}.

Similarly, although
\bar{{\bm{a}}}_{\ell}
depends on
{\bm{x}}
for all
\ell=0,\cdots,L-1
, we omit
{\bm{x}}
in the notation.

Let us denote the difference of parameters as
\Delta{\bm{W}}_{\ell}:=\bar{{\bm{W}}}_{\ell}-{\bm{W}}_{\ell}
,
\Delta{\bm{b}}_{\ell}:=\bar{{\bm{b}}}_{\ell}-{\bm{b}}_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[L]
and the difference of layer outputs
\Delta{\bm{a}}_{\ell}:=\bar{{\bm{a}}}_{\ell}-{\bm{a}}_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[L-1]
.
It is straightforward to check

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{F}\leq\sqrt{d_{l}d_{l-1}}M\leq DM

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\bm{b}}_{\ell}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{d_{l}}M\leq\sqrt{D}M

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{F}\leq\sqrt{d_{l}d_{l-1}}\zeta\leq D\zeta

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{b}}_{\ell}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{d_{l}}\zeta\leq\sqrt{D}\zeta,

where the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{F}
for the matrix denote the spectral norm and the Frobenius norm, respectively.

We first claim that there exists a degree
2L+1
polynomial
S
on
D,M,L
and
R
such that
\left\lVert{\bm{a}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{2}\leq S
for all
\ell\in[L-1]
.

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\bm{a}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sigma({\bm{W}}_{\ell}{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}+{\bm{b}}_{\ell})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert{\bm{W}}_{\ell}{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}+{\bm{b}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\right\rVert\left\lVert{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert{\bm{b}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq DM\left\lVert{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}\right\rVert_{2}+\sqrt{D}M.

Thus for all
\ell\in[L-1]
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\bm{a}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sqrt{D}M\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in[\ell]}(DM)^{\ell^{\prime}-1}\right)+(DM)^{l}\left\lVert{\bm{a}}_{0}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{D}ML(DM)^{L-1}+(DM)^{L-1}R

\displaystyle\leq(DM)^{L-1}(DML+R)=:S

This proves the first claim.
Moreover,
S
is composed of two monomials whose coefficients are all 1.

We next claim that the error
\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{L-1}\right\rVert\leq Q\zeta
for some degree
4L+1
polynomial
Q
on
D,M,L
and
R
.
Consider the following recurrence

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\bar{{\bm{a}}}_{\ell}-{\bm{a}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sigma(\bar{{\bm{W}}}_{\ell}\bar{{\bm{a}}}_{\ell-1}+\bar{{\bm{b}}}_{\ell})-\sigma({\bm{W}}_{\ell}{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}+{\bm{b}}_{\ell})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\bar{{\bm{W}}}_{\ell}\bar{{\bm{a}}}_{\ell-1}+\bar{{\bm{b}}}_{\ell})-({\bm{W}}_{\ell}{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}+{\bm{b}}_{\ell})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(({\bm{W}}_{\ell}+\Delta{\bm{W}}_{\ell})({\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}+\Delta{\bm{a}}_{\ell})+({\bm{b}}_{\ell}+\Delta{\bm{b}}_{\ell}))-({\bm{W}}_{\ell}{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}+{\bm{b}}_{\ell})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{W}}_{\ell}{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}+{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\Delta{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}+\Delta{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\Delta{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}+\Delta{\bm{b}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\right\rVert\left\lVert{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\right\rVert\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{W}}_{\ell}\right\rVert\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{b}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq D\zeta\left\lVert{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}\right\rVert_{2}+DM\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}\right\rVert_{2}+D\zeta\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}\right\rVert_{2}+\sqrt{D}\zeta

\displaystyle=(DM+D\zeta)\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}\right\rVert_{2}+(D\left\lVert{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}\right\rVert_{2}+\sqrt{D})\zeta

\displaystyle\leq(DM+D\zeta)\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{\ell-1}\right\rVert_{2}+(DS+\sqrt{D})\zeta.

Thus noting that
\Delta{\bm{a}}_{0}={\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}=0
we have,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{L-1}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(DS+\sqrt{D}\right)\zeta\sum_{\ell\in[L-1]}(DM+D\zeta)^{\ell-1}

\displaystyle\leq\left(DS+\sqrt{D}\right)\zeta\times L(DM+D\zeta)^{L-1}

\displaystyle\leq DL(S+1)(DM+D\zeta)^{L-1}\zeta

\displaystyle\leq DL(S+1)(2DM)^{L-1}\zeta,

where the last inequality follows from
\zeta\leq M
.
Let
Q:=DL(S+1)(2DM)^{L-1}
.
Since
S
is a degree
2L+1
polynomial on
D,M,L
and
R
, it follows that
Q
is a degree
4L+1
polynomial on
D,M,L
and
R
.
This proves the second claim.
Moreover,
Q
is composed of three monomials whose coefficients are at most
2^{L}
.

Thus,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bar{f}({\bm{x}})-f({\bm{x}})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\bar{{\bm{W}}}_{L}\bar{{\bm{a}}}_{L-1}+\bar{{\bm{b}}}_{L})-{\bm{W}}_{L}{\bm{a}}_{L-1}+{\bm{b}}_{L}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(({\bm{W}}_{L}+\Delta{\bm{W}}_{L})({\bm{a}}_{L-1}+\Delta{\bm{a}}_{L-1})+({\bm{b}}_{L}+\Delta{\bm{b}}_{L}))-({\bm{W}}_{L}{\bm{a}}_{L-1}+{\bm{b}}_{L})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{W}}_{L}{\bm{a}}_{L-1}+{\bm{W}}_{L}\Delta{\bm{a}}_{L-1}+\Delta{\bm{W}}_{L}\Delta{\bm{a}}_{L-1}+\Delta{\bm{b}}_{L}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{W}}_{L}\right\rVert\left\lVert{\bm{a}}_{L-1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert{\bm{W}}_{L}\right\rVert\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{L-1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{W}}_{L}\right\rVert\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{L-1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{b}}_{L}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq D\zeta\left\lVert{\bm{a}}_{L-1}\right\rVert_{2}+DM\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{L-1}\right\rVert_{2}+D\zeta\left\lVert\Delta{\bm{a}}_{L-1}\right\rVert_{2}+\sqrt{D}\zeta

\displaystyle\leq D\zeta S+DMQ\zeta+D\zeta Q\zeta+D\zeta

\displaystyle\leq(DS+DMQ+DMQ+D)\zeta,

where we use
\zeta\leq M
in the last inequality.
Now, by letting
\zeta:=\frac{\nu}{DS+2DMQ+D}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bar{f}({\bm{x}})-f({\bm{x}})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\nu,

for all
{\bm{x}}
with
\left\lVert{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{2}\leq R
.
Thus, it suffices to have
\log_{2}(M/\zeta)=\log_{2}((DS+2DMQ+D)M/\nu)
bit complexity to attain an approximation of accuracy
\nu
uniformly over the bounded domain with radius
R
.
(DS+2DMQ+D)M
is a degree
4L+4
polynomial on
D,M,L
and
R
.
Moreover, it is composed of 2 + 3 + 1 = 6 monomials, whose coefficients are at most
2^{L+1}
. Hence, it follows that

\displaystyle\log_{2}(M/\zeta)

\displaystyle=\log_{2}((DS+2DMQ+D)M/\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\log_{2}(6\times 2^{L+1}\times(DMLR)^{4L+4})+\log(1/\nu)

\displaystyle=O(L\log_{2}(2DMLR)+\log(1/\nu))

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}(L)

bit complexity suffices.
∎

Appendix C
Extensions to
\ell_{p}
-norm

In this section, we extend the previous results on
\ell_{2}
-norm to arbitrary
p
-norm, where
p\in[1,\infty]
.

In the following, we use
\mathrm{dist}_{p}(\cdot,\cdot)
to denote the
\ell_{p}
-norm distance between two points, a point and a set, or two sets.
For the case
d=1
, we omit the notation
p
since every
\ell_{p}
-norm in 1-dimension denotes the absolute value.

We denote
{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}},\mu)=\left\{{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\middle|\|{\bm{x}}^{\prime}-{\bm{x}}\|_{p}<\mu\right\}
an open
\ell_{p}
-ball centered at
{\bm{x}}
with a radius
\mu
.

Definition C.1
.

For
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
, the separation constant
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm is defined as

\displaystyle\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\|{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j}\|_{p}\,\middle|\,({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i}),({\bm{x}}_{j},y_{j})\in{\mathcal{D}},\ y_{i}\neq y_{j}\right\}.

As we consider
{\mathcal{D}}
with
{\bm{x}}_{i}\neq{\bm{x}}_{j}
for all
i\neq j
, we have
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}>0
.
Next, we define robust memorization under
\ell_{p}
-norm.

Definition C.2
.

For
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
, and a given
robustness ratio

\rho\in(0,1)
, define the robustness radius as
\mu=\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}
. We say that a function
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

\rho
-
robustly memorizes

{\mathcal{D}}
under the
\ell_{p}
-norm if

\displaystyle f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})=y_{i},\quad\text{
for all }({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\in{\mathcal{D}}\text{
and }{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu),

and
{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu)
is referred as the
robustness ball
of
{\bm{x}}_{i}
.

Similarly, we extend the notion of
\rho
-robust memorization error to
\ell_{p}
-norm.

Definition C.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
be a class(or point)-separated dataset. The
\rho
-robust error of a network
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
on
{\mathcal{D}}
under the
\ell_{p}
-norm is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rho,p}(f,{\mathcal{D}})=\max_{({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\in{\mathcal{D}}}\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\sim\text{Unif}({\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu))}[f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\neq y_{i}],\textrm{ where }\mu=\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}\textrm{ (or }\mu=\rho\epsilon^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{D}},p}).

The following inclusion between
p
-norm balls with different
p
-values is well known.

Lemma C.4

(Inclusion Between Balls)
.

Let
0<p<q\leq\infty
. Then, for any
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\mu>0
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}},\mu)\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{q}({\bm{x}},\mu)\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}},d^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}\mu),

or equivalently,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{q}({\bm{x}},d^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}}\mu)\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}},\mu)\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{q}({\bm{x}},\mu).

For any
p\in[1,\infty]
, let us denote

\gamma_{p}(d):=d^{\left|\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\right|}

throughout this section.
For
0<p<q\leq\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},q}\leq\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}\leq d^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},q},

(60)

since
\left\lVert{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{q}\leq\left\lVert{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{p}\leq d^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}\left\lVert{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{q}
.
In particular, we have

\displaystyle\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}

\displaystyle\textrm{ when }p\geq 2,

(61)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{p}(d)\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}

\displaystyle\textrm{ when }p<2.

(62)

C.1
Extension of Necessity Condition to
\ell_{p}
-norm

Theorem C.5
.

Let
\rho\in(0,1)
. Suppose for any
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,2}
, there exists a neural network
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
that can
\rho
-robustly memorize
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm.
Then, the number of parameters
P
must satisfy

•

P=\Omega\left(\left(\rho^{2}\min\{N,d\}+1\right)d+\min\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\rho^{p}}},\sqrt{d}\right\}\sqrt{N}\right)
if
p\geq 2
.

•

P=\Omega\left(\left(\left(\frac{\rho}{\gamma_{p}(d)}\right)^{2}\min\{N,d\}+1\right)d+\min\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\rho^{p}}},\sqrt{d}\right\}\sqrt{N}\right)
if
1\leq p<2
.

Proof.

This follows by combining
Proposition

C.6
and
Proposition

C.8
.
∎

Proposition C.6
.

There exists
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,2}
such that any neural network
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
that
\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm must have the first hidden layer width at least

•

\rho^{2}\min\{N-1,d\}
if
p\geq 2
.

•

\left(\frac{\rho}{\gamma_{p}(d)}\right)^{2}\min\{N-1,d\}
if
1\leq p<2
.

Proof.

We take
{\mathcal{D}}
the same dataset as in
Proposition

3.2
.
Recall that in the proof of
Proposition

3.2
, we take the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}=\{{\bm{e}}_{j},2\}_{j\in[N-1]}\cup\{{\bm{0}},1\}
when
N\leq d+1
, with additional data points
(2{\bm{e}}_{1},2),(3{\bm{e}}_{1},2),\cdots,((N-d){\bm{e}}_{1},2)
when
N>d+1
.
This has a separation
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}=\frac{1}{2}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm for all
p\geq 1
, on the both case
N\leq d+1
and
N>d+1
.
Let
f
be a neural network that robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm.
Since
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}=\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}
, the robustness radius
\mu
under
\ell_{2}
-norm satisfies
\mu=\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}=\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}
.
With this in mind, we now prove the proposition.
The statement of the proposition consists of two parts,
p\geq 2
and
1\leq p<2
.

Part I:
p\geq 2
.

First, we prove the result under
p\geq 2

Robust memorization under
\ell_{p}
-norm implies

\displaystyle f({\bm{x}})=y_{i}\textrm{ for all }({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\in{\mathcal{D}}\textrm{ and }{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu),

where
\mu=\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}=\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}
.
For
p\geq 2
, we have
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu)\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu)
by
Lemma

C.4
.
Thus,

\displaystyle f({\bm{x}})=y_{i}\textrm{ for all }({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\in{\mathcal{D}}\textrm{ and }{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu).

Since
\mu=\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}
this implies that
f

\rho
-robustly memorize
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{2}
-norm.
By
Proposition

3.2
,
f
should have the first hidden layer width at least
\rho^{2}\min\{N-1,d\}
.

Part II:
1\leq p<2
.

Next, we prove the result under
1\leq p<2
.
Robust memorization under
\ell_{p}
-norm implies

\displaystyle f({\bm{x}})=y_{i}\textrm{ for all }({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\in{\mathcal{D}}\textrm{ and }{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu),

where
\mu=\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}=\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}
.
For
1\leq p<2
, we have
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}},d^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\mu)\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu)
by applying
p=p
and
q=2
to
Lemma

C.4
.
Since
\gamma_{p}(d)=d^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}
, we have
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu/\gamma_{p}(d))\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\mu)
.
In particular,
f
memorize every
\mu/\gamma_{p}(d)
neighbor around the data point under
\ell_{2}
-norm.
Let

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}:=\frac{\mu/\gamma_{p}(d)}{\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}}=\frac{\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}/\gamma_{p}(d)}{\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}}=\frac{\rho}{\gamma_{p}(d)}

Then,
f
memorize every
\mu/\gamma_{p}(d)=\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}
radius neighbor around each data point under
\ell_{2}
-norm.
In other words,
f

\rho^{\prime}
-robustly memorize
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{2}
-norm.
By
Proposition

3.2
,
f
should have the first hidden layer width at least
(\rho^{\prime})^{2}\min\{N-1,d\}
. Putting back
\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\rho}{\gamma_{p}(d)}
concludes the desired statement.

∎

Proposition C.7
.

There exists a point separated
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,2}
such that any neural network that
\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{\infty}
-norm must have the first hidden layer width at least

•

\rho^{2}\min\{d,N-1\}
if
\rho\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
.

•

\min\{d,N-1\}
if
\rho\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
.

Proof.

The first bullet is an immediate corollary of
Proposition

C.6
, so we focus on the second bullet for
\rho\in(1/2,1)
.
To prove the second bullet, we consider two cases based on the relationship between
N-1
and
d
. In the first case, where
N-1\leq d
, establishing the proposition requires that the first hidden layer has width at least
N-1
. In the second case, where
N-1>d
, the required width is at least
d
. For each case, we construct a dataset
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,2}
such that any network that
\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
must have a first hidden layer of width no smaller than the corresponding bound.

Case I :
N-1\leq d
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}=\{({\bm{e}}_{j},2)\}_{j\in[N-1]}\cup\{({\bm{0}},1)\}
.
Then,
{\mathcal{D}}
has a separation constant
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},\infty}=1/2
under
\ell_{\infty}
-norm.
Let
f
be a
\rho
-robust memorizer of
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{\infty}
-norm whose first hidden layer width is
m
.
Let
{\bm{W}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times d}
denote the first hidden weight matrix.
Suppose for a contradiction,
m<N-1
.

Let
\mu=\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},\infty}
denote the robustness radius.
Then,
f
has to distinguish every point in each
B_{\mu}({\bm{e}}_{j})
from every point in
B_{\mu}({\bm{0}})
for all
j\in[N-1]
.
Therefore, for
{\bm{x}}\in B_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},\mu)
and
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in B_{\infty}({\bm{0}},\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle{\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}\neq{\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}^{\prime},

or equivalently,
{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\notin{\mathrm{Null}}({\bm{W}})
.
Moreover

\displaystyle B_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},\mu)-B_{\infty}({\bm{0}},\mu):=\{{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}:{\bm{x}}\in B_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},\mu)\textrm{ and }{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in B_{\infty}({\bm{0}},\mu)\}=B_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},2\mu).

Hence, it is necessary to have
B_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},2\mu)\cap{\mathrm{Null}}({\bm{W}})=\emptyset
for all
j\in[N-1]
, or equivalently,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},{\mathrm{Null}}({\bm{W}}))\geq 2\mu

(63)

for all
j\in[N-1]
.

Since
\dim{\mathrm{Col}}(W^{\top})\leq\dim{\mathbb{R}}^{m}=m
, we have
\dim{\mathrm{Null}}(W)\geq d-m
.
Using
Lemma

C.10
, we can upper bounds the maximum possible distance between
\{{\bm{e}}_{j}\}_{j\in[N-1]}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and arbitrary subspace of a fixed dimension.

Take
Z\subseteq{\mathrm{Null}}(W)
such that
\dim Z=d-m
and substitute
d=d
,
t=N-1
,
k=d-m
and
Z=Z
into
Lemma

C.10
.
The assumptions
t\leq d
for the lemma are satisfied since
N-1\leq d
.
The additional assumption
k\geq d-t+1
is equivalent to
d-m\geq d-(N-1)+1
and is satisfied since
m<N-1
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\min_{j\in[N-1]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},Z)\leq\frac{1}{2}.

By combining the above inequality with
Equation

63
,

\displaystyle 2\mu\leq\min_{j\in[N-1]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},{\mathrm{Null}}(W))\overset{(a)}{\leq}\min_{j\in[N-1]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},Z)\leq\frac{1}{2},

(64)

where (a) is due to
Z\subseteq{\mathrm{Null}}(W)
.
Since
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},\infty}=1/2
, we have
2\mu=2\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},\infty}=\rho
so that
Equation

64
becomes
\rho\leq 1/2
.
This contradicts our assumption
\rho\in(1/2,1)
, and therefore the width requirement
m\geq N-1
is necessary.
This concludes the proof for the case
N-1\leq d
.

Case II :
N-1>d
.

We construct the first
d+1
data points in the same manner as in Case I, using the construction for
N=d+1
.
For the remaining
N-d-1
data points, we set them sufficiently distant from the first
d+1
data points to keep
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},\infty}=1/2
.
In particular, we can set
{\bm{x}}_{d+2}=2{\bm{e}}_{1},{\bm{x}}_{d+3}=3{\bm{e}}_{1},\cdots,{\bm{x}}_{N}=(N-d){\bm{e}}_{1}
and
y_{d+2}=y_{d+3}=\cdots=y_{N}=2
.
Compared to the case
N=d+1
, we have
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},\infty}
unchanged while having more data points to memorize.
By the necessity for the case
N=d+1
, this dataset also requires the first hidden layer width at least
(d+1)-1=d
.
This concludes the statement for the case
N-1>d
.

Combining the result of the two cases
N-1\leq d
and
N-1>d
concludes the proof of the theorem.

∎

Proposition C.8
.

For
p\in[1,\infty)
, let
\rho\in\left(0,\left(1-\frac{1}{d}\right)^{1/p}\right]
.
Suppose for any
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,2}
there exists
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
that
\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm.
Then, the number of parameters
P
must satisfy
P=\Omega(\sqrt{\frac{N}{1-\rho^{p}}})
.

Proof.

The main idea of the proof is the same as
Proposition

3.3
.
We construct
\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor\times\lfloor\frac{1}{1-\rho^{p}}\rfloor
number of data points that can be shattered by
{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
.
This proves
\mathrm{VC\text{-}dim}({\mathcal{F}}_{d,P})\geq\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor\times\lfloor\frac{1}{1-\rho^{p}}\rfloor=\Omega(N/(1-\rho^{p}))
.
Since
\mathrm{VC\text{-}dim}({\mathcal{F}}_{d,P})=O(P^{2})
, this proves
P=\Omega(\sqrt{N/(1-\rho^{p})})
.

For simplicity of the notation, let us denote
k:=\lfloor\frac{1}{1-\rho^{p}}\rfloor
.
To prove the lower bound on the VC-dimension, we construct
k\times\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor
points in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
that can be shattered by
{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
.
As in the proof of
Proposition

3.3
, we define
\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor\times k
number of points as
\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor
groups, where each group consists of
k
points.

We start by constructing the first group.
Since
\rho\in(0,\left(\frac{d-1}{d}\right)^{1/p}]
, we have
k=\lfloor\frac{1}{1-\rho^{p}}\rfloor\in[1,d]
.
The first group
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}:=\{{\bm{e}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is defined as the set of the first
k
vectors in the standard basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
The remaining
\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor-1
groups are simply constructed as a translation of
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
.
In particular, for
l\in[\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor]
, we define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{l}:={\bm{c}}_{l}+{\mathcal{X}}_{1}=\left\{{\bm{c}}_{l}+{\bm{x}}\,\mid\,{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\right\}

where
{\bm{c}}_{l}:=2d^{2}(l-1)\times{\bm{e}}_{1}
ensures that each group is sufficiently far from one another.
Note that
{\bm{c}}_{1}={\bm{0}}
ensures
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
also satisfies the consistency of the notation.
Now, define
{\mathcal{X}}=\cup_{l\in[\lfloor N/2\rfloor]}{\mathcal{X}}_{l}
, the union of all
\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor
groups which consists of
k\times\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor
points.

We claim that if for any
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,2}
, there exists
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
that
\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm, then
{\mathcal{X}}
is shattered by
{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
.
To prove the claim, suppose we are given arbitrary label
{\mathcal{Y}}=\{y_{l,j}\}_{l\in[\lfloor N/2\rfloor],j\in[d]}
of
{\mathcal{X}}
, where
y_{l,j}\in\{\pm 1\}
denotes the label for
{\bm{x}}_{l,j}:={\bm{c}}_{l}+{\bm{e}}_{j}\in{\mathcal{X}}
.
Given the label
{\mathcal{Y}}
, we construct
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,2}
such that whenever
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}

\rho
-robustly memorize
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm, then its affine translation
f^{\prime}=2f-3\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
satisfies
f^{\prime}({\bm{x}}_{l,j})=y_{l,j}
for all
{\bm{x}}_{l,j}\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

For each
l\in[\lfloor N/2\rfloor]
, let
J_{l}^{+}=\left\{j\in[k]\,\mid\,y_{l,j}=+1\right\}
and
J_{l}^{-}=\left\{j\in[k]\,\mid\,y_{l,j}=-1\right\}
.
Define

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}_{2l-1}

\displaystyle={\bm{c}}_{l}+\sum_{j\in J_{l}^{+}}{\bm{e}}_{j}-\sum_{j\in J_{l}^{-}}{\bm{e}}_{j}

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}_{2l}

\displaystyle={\bm{c}}_{l}+\sum_{j\in J_{l}^{-}}{\bm{e}}_{j}-\sum_{j\in J_{l}^{+}}{\bm{e}}_{j}

Furthermore, define
y_{2l-1}=2,y_{2l}=1
and let
{\mathcal{D}}=\{({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,2}
.
To consider the separation
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}
, notice that

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{2l-1}-{\bm{x}}_{2l}\right\rVert_{p}=\left\lVert 2\left(\sum_{j\in J_{l}^{+}}{\bm{e}}_{j}-\sum_{j\in J_{l}^{-}}{\bm{e}}_{j}\right)\right\rVert_{p}\overset{(a)}{=}2k^{1/p},

where (a) is due to
J_{l}^{+}\cap J_{l}^{-}=\emptyset
and
J_{l}^{+}\cup J_{l}^{-}=[k]
.
For
l\neq l^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle d_{p}({\bm{x}}_{2l-1},{\bm{x}}_{2l^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}d_{p}({\bm{c}}_{l},{\bm{c}}_{l^{\prime}})-d_{p}({\bm{c}}_{l},{\bm{x}}_{2l-1})-d_{p}({\bm{c}}_{l^{\prime}},{\bm{x}}_{2l^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}2d^{2}-k^{1/p}-k^{1/p}

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\geq}2d^{2}-2d^{1/p}

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\geq}2d^{1/p}

\displaystyle\overset{(e)}{\geq}2k^{1/p},

where (a) is by the triangle inequality under
\ell_{p}
-norm (namely, the Minkowski inequality), (b) uses
d_{p}({\bm{c}}_{l},{\bm{x}}_{2l-1})=d_{p}({\bm{c}}_{l^{\prime}},{\bm{x}}_{2l^{\prime}})=k^{1/p}
, (c),(e) is by
k\leq d
, and (d) holds for all
d\geq 2
and
p\geq 1
.
Thus, we have
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}\geq k^{1/p}
.

Take
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
that
\rho
-robustly memorize
{\mathcal{D}}
.
We first lower bound the robustness radius
\mu
.
Since
t\overset{\phi}{\mapsto}\sqrt[p]{\frac{t-1}{t}}
is an strictly increasing function from
t\geq 1
onto
[0,1)

2
2

2

\phi
is a composition of two strictly increasing one-to-one corresponding functions
t\mapsto\frac{t-1}{t}
from
[1,\infty)
onto
[0,1)
and
u\mapsto\sqrt[p]{u}
from
[0,1)
onto
[0,1)

, it has a well defined inverse mapping
\phi^{-1}:[0,1)\rightarrow[1,\infty)
defined as
\phi^{-1}(\rho)=\frac{1}{1-\rho^{p}}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\rho=\phi(\phi^{-1}(\rho))=\phi\left(\frac{1}{1-\rho^{p}}\right)\geq\phi\left(\lfloor\frac{1}{1-\rho^{p}}\rfloor\right)=\phi(k)=\sqrt[p]{\frac{k-1}{k}}.

Since
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}\geq k^{1/p}
and
\rho\geq(\frac{k-1}{k})^{1/p}
, we have
\mu=\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}\geq\rho k^{1/p}\geq(k-1)^{1/p}
.
Thus, every
f
that
\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
must also memorize
(k-1)^{1/p}
radius open
\ell_{p}
-ball around each point in
{\mathcal{D}}
as the same label as the data point.

Moreover, for
{\bm{x}}_{l,j}\in{\mathcal{X}}
with positive label
y_{l,j}=+1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{l,j}-{\bm{x}}_{2l-1}\right\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert({\bm{c}}_{l}+{\bm{e}}_{j})-({\bm{c}}_{l}+\sum_{j^{\prime}\in J_{l}^{+}}{\bm{e}}_{j^{\prime}}-\sum_{j^{\prime}\in J_{l}^{-}}{\bm{e}}_{j^{\prime}})\right\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in J_{l}^{+}\\
j^{\prime}\neq j\end{subarray}}{\bm{e}}_{j^{\prime}}-\sum_{j^{\prime}\in J_{l}^{-}}{\bm{e}}_{j^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle=(k-1)^{1/p}.

Take a sequence of points
\{{\bm{z}}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that
{\bm{z}}_{n}\rightarrow{\bm{x}}_{l,j}
as
n\rightarrow\infty

3
3

3

We consider the convergence of the sequence on the usual topology induced by
\ell_{2}
-norm.

and

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\bm{z}}_{n}-{\bm{x}}_{2l-1}\right\rVert_{p}<(k-1)^{1/p},

for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
In particular,

\displaystyle{\bm{z}}_{n}:=\frac{n-1}{n}{\bm{x}}_{l,j}+\frac{1}{n}{\bm{x}}_{2l-1}

satisfies such properties.
Then, we have
f({\bm{z}}_{n})=f({\bm{x}}_{2l-1})=2
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Moreover, by the continuity of
f
(under the usual topology),

\displaystyle f({\bm{x}}_{l,j})=f(\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\bm{z}}_{n})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f({\bm{z}}_{n})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}2=2.

Similarly, for
{\bm{x}}_{l,j}
with negative label
y_{l,j}=-1
, we have
\left\lVert{\bm{x}}_{l,j}-{\bm{x}}_{2l}\right\rVert_{p}=(k-1)^{1/p}
, so that
f({\bm{x}}_{l,j})=1
.

Since we can adjust the weight and the bias of the last hidden layer,
{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
is closed under affine transformation; that is,
af+b\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
whenever
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
.
In particular,
f^{\prime}:=2f-3\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
.
This
f^{\prime}
satisfies
f^{\prime}({\bm{x}}_{l,j})=2f({\bm{x}}_{l,j})-3=2\cdot 2-3=+1
whenever
y_{l,j}=+1
and
f^{\prime}({\bm{x}}_{l,j})=2f({\bm{x}}_{l,j})-3=2\cdot 1-3=-1
whenever
y_{l,j}=-1
.
Thus,
\sign\circ f^{\prime}
perfectly classify
{\mathcal{X}}
with the label
{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Since we can take such
f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
given an arbitrary label
{\mathcal{Y}}
of
{\mathcal{X}}
, it follows that
{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
shatters
{\mathcal{X}}
, concluding the proof of the theorem.
∎

C.1.1
Lemmas for
Section

C.1

Lemma C.9
.

Let
\{{\bm{e}}_{j}\}_{j\in[d]}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
denote the standard basis in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Then, for any
k
-dimensional subspace
Z
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with
k\geq 1
we have,

\displaystyle\min_{j\in[d]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},Z)\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Proof.

For any subspace
Z^{\prime}
of
Z
, we have

\displaystyle\min_{j\in[d]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},Z)\leq\min_{j\in[d]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},Z^{\prime}).

As every
k
-dimensional subspace of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with
k\geq 1
has a one-dimensional subspace, it suffices to prove the second statement for
k=1
. i.e., for any one-dimensional subspace
Z
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\min_{j\in[d]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},Z)\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Let
Z=\textrm{Span}({\bm{z}})
, where
{\bm{z}}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})\neq{\bm{0}}
. Without loss of generality, let
\left\lVert{\bm{z}}\right\rVert_{\infty}=1
and take
j\in[d]
such that
\left|z_{j}\right|=1
. Let
{\bm{z}}^{\prime}=\frac{z_{j}}{2}{\bm{z}}\in Z
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\bm{z}}^{\prime}-{\bm{e}}_{j}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\frac{z_{j}z_{1}}{2},\cdots,\frac{z_{j}z_{j-1}}{2},\frac{z_{j}z_{j}}{2}-1,\frac{z_{j}z_{j+1}}{2},\cdots,\frac{z_{j}z_{d}}{2})\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\left\lVert(\frac{z_{j}z_{1}}{2},\cdots,\frac{z_{j}z_{j-1}}{2},-\frac{1}{2},\frac{z_{j}z_{j+1}}{2},\cdots,\frac{z_{j}z_{d}}{2})\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2},

where (a) is by
\left|z_{j}\right|=1
, and (b) is by
\left\lVert{\bm{z}}\right\rVert_{\infty}=1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\min_{j^{\prime}\in[d]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j^{\prime}},Z)\leq\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},Z)\leq\left\lVert{\bm{z}}^{\prime}-{\bm{e}}_{j}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2},

concluding the statement.
∎

The following lemma generalizes
Lemma

C.9
to the case where we consider only the distance to a subset of the standard basis, instead of the whole standard basis.

Lemma C.10
.

For
1\leq t\leq d
, let
\{{\bm{e}}_{j}\}_{j\in[t]}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
denote the first
t
vectors from the standard basis in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Then, for any
k
-dimensional subspace
Z
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with
k\geq d-t+1
,

\displaystyle\min_{j\in[t]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},Z)\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Proof.

Similar to
Lemma

A.2
, we start by considering the dimension of the intersection between
Z
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{t}
, both as a subspace of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Let
Q=[{\bm{e}}_{1}{\bm{e}}_{2}\cdots{\bm{e}}_{t}]^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t\times d}
. Then,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{d}={\mathrm{Col}}(Q^{\top})\oplus{\mathrm{Null}}(Q)=(Z\cap{\mathrm{Col}}(Q^{\top}))\oplus(Z^{\perp}\cap{\mathrm{Col}}(Q^{\top}))\oplus{\mathrm{Null}}(Q).

By considering the dimension,

\displaystyle\dim(Z\cap{\mathrm{Col}}(Q^{\top}))

\displaystyle=\dim{\mathbb{R}}^{d}-\dim(Z^{\perp}\cap{\mathrm{Col}}(Q^{\top}))-\dim{\mathrm{Null}}(Q)

\displaystyle\geq\dim{\mathbb{R}}^{d}-\dim Z^{\perp}-\dim{\mathrm{Null}}(Q)

\displaystyle=d-(d-k)-(d-t)

\displaystyle=k-(d-t)

Under the assumption
k\geq d-t+1
, we have

\displaystyle\dim\phi(Z\cap{\mathrm{Col}}(Q^{\top})=\dim(Z\cap{\mathrm{Col}}(Q^{\top})\geq k-(d-t)\geq 1.

Then,

\displaystyle\min_{j\in[t]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},Z)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{j\in[t]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}({\bm{e}}_{j},Z\cap{\mathrm{Col}}(Q^{\top}))

\displaystyle=\min_{j\in[t]}\mathrm{dist}_{\infty}(\phi({\bm{e}}_{j}),\phi(Z\cap{\mathrm{Col}}(Q^{\top}))

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2},

where (b) is by
Lemma

C.9
.
∎

C.2
Extension of Sufficiency Condition to
\ell_{p}
-norm

Theorem C.11
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
.
For any dataset
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
and
\eta\in(0,1)
, the following statements hold:

(i)

If
\rho\in\Big(0,\frac{1}{5N\sqrt{d}\gamma_{p}(d)}\Big]
, there exists
f\in\mathcal{F}_{d,P}
with
P=\tilde{O}(\sqrt{N})
that
\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm.

(ii)

If
\rho\in\Big(\frac{1}{5N\sqrt{d}\gamma_{p}(d)},\frac{1}{5\sqrt{d}\gamma_{p}(d)}\Big]
, there exists
f\in\mathcal{F}_{d,P}
with
P=\tilde{O}(Nd^{\frac{1}{4}}\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}\gamma_{p}(d)^{\frac{1}{2}})
that
\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm with error at most
\eta
.

(iii)

If
\rho\in\Big(\frac{1}{5\sqrt{d}\gamma_{p}(d)},\frac{1}{\gamma_{p}(d)}\Big)
, there exists
f\in\mathcal{F}_{d,P}
with
P=\tilde{O}(Nd^{2}\rho^{4}\gamma_{p}(d)^{4})
that
\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm.

To prove
Theorem

C.11
, we decompose it into three theorems (
Theorems

C.12
,
C.13
and
C.14
), each corresponding to one of the cases in the statement. They are following.

Theorem C.12
.

Let
\rho\in\Big(0,\frac{1}{5N\sqrt{d}\gamma_{p}(d)}\Big]
and
p\in[1,\infty]
.
For any dataset
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
, there exists
f\in\mathcal{F}_{d,P}
with
P=\tilde{O}(\sqrt{N})
that
\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm.

Proof.

Let
\rho^{\prime}=\gamma_{p}(d)\rho
. Then, we have
\rho^{\prime}\in\Big(0,\frac{1}{5N\sqrt{d}}\Big]
from the condition of
\rho
.
By Theorem
4.2(i)
, there exists
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
with
P=\tilde{O}(\sqrt{N})
that
\rho^{\prime}
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{2}
-norm. In other words, it holds
f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})=y_{i}
, for all
({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2})
.

We consider two cases depending on whether
p\geq 2
or
p<2
, which affect the direction of inclusion between
\ell_{p}
and
\ell_{2}
balls.

Case I :
p\geq 2
.

In this case, we have

{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p})\overset{(a)}{\subseteq}{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2})\overset{(b)}{\subseteq}{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\gamma_{p}(d)\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2})={\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}),

where (a) holds by
Equation

61
and (b) holds by
Lemma

C.4
applying
p=2
and
q=p
.

Thus, for all
({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p})
, it also holds
f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})=y_{i}
. In other words,
f

\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm with
\tilde{O}(\sqrt{N})
parameters.

Case II :
p<2
.

In this case, we have

{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p})\overset{(a)}{\subseteq}{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\gamma_{p}(d)\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2})\overset{(b)}{\subseteq}{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\gamma_{p}(d)\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2})={\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}),

where (a) holds by
Equation

62
and (b) holds by
Lemma

C.4
applying
p=p
and
q=2
.

Thus, for all
({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p})
, it also holds
f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})=y_{i}
. In other words,
f

\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm with
\tilde{O}(\sqrt{N})
parameters.

∎

Theorem C.13
.

Let
\rho\in\Big(\frac{1}{5N\sqrt{d}\gamma_{p}(d)},\frac{1}{5\sqrt{d}\gamma_{p}(d)}\Big]
and
p\in[1,\infty]
.
For any dataset
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\bm{D}}_{d,N,C}
, there exists
f\in\mathcal{F}_{d,P}
with
P=\tilde{O}(Nd^{\frac{1}{4}}\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}\gamma_{p}(d)^{\frac{1}{2}})
that
\rho
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
under
\ell_{p}
-norm with error at most
\eta
.

Proof.

Let
\rho^{\prime}=\gamma_{p}(d)\rho
. Then, we have
\rho^{\prime}\in\Big(\frac{1}{5N\sqrt{d}},\frac{1}{5\sqrt{d}}\Big)
from the condition of
\rho
.

We consider two cases depending on whether
p\geq 2
or
p<2
, which affect the direction of inclusion between
\ell_{p}
and
\ell_{2}
balls.

Case I :
p\geq 2
.

In this case, we have:

{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p})\overset{(a)}{\subseteq}{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2})\overset{(b)}{\subseteq}{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\gamma_{p}(d)\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2})={\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}),

where (a) holds by
Equation

61
and (b) holds by
Lemma

C.4
applying
p=2
and
q=p
.

Case II :
p<2
.

In this case, we have:

{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p})\overset{(a)}{\subseteq}{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\gamma_{p}(d)\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2})\overset{(b)}{\subseteq}{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\gamma_{p}(d)\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2})={\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}),

where (a) holds by
Equation

62
and (b) holds by
Lemma

C.4
applying
p=p
and
q=2
.

Thus, in both cases, it holds:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p})\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}).

(65)

We define
\eta^{\prime}=\eta\frac{\operatorname{Vol}({\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}))}{\operatorname{Vol}({\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2})}
.
We apply Theorem
4.2(ii)
with the robustness ratio
\rho^{\prime}
and the error rate
\eta^{\prime}
, then we obtain
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{d,P}
with
P=\tilde{O}(Nd^{\frac{1}{4}}\rho^{\prime\frac{1}{2}})=\tilde{O}(Nd^{\frac{1}{4}}\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}\gamma_{p}(d)^{\frac{1}{2}})
that
\rho^{\prime}
-robustly memorizes
{\mathcal{D}}
with error at most
\eta^{\prime}
under
\ell_{2}
-norm. In other words, for all
({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\in{\mathcal{D}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\sim\text{Unif}({\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}))}[f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\neq y_{i}]<\eta^{\prime}.

(66)

For simplicity, we denote
E=\{{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\neq y_{i}\}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\sim\text{Unif}({\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}))}[f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\neq y_{i}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\sim\text{Unif}({\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}))}[{\bm{x}}\in E]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{Vol}(E\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}))}{\operatorname{Vol}({\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}))}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{Vol}(E\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}))}{\operatorname{Vol}({\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{Vol}(E\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}))}{\operatorname{Vol}({\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}))}\frac{\operatorname{Vol}({\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}))}{\operatorname{Vol}({\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\sim\text{Unif}({\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}))}[{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in E]\cdot\frac{\operatorname{Vol}({\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}))}{\operatorname{Vol}({\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\sim\text{Unif}({\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}))}[f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\neq y_{i}]\cdot\frac{\operatorname{Vol}({\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho^{\prime}\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},2}))}{\operatorname{Vol}({\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\bm{x}}_{i},\rho\epsilon_{{\mathcal{D}},p}))}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{<}
